#128. Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

This month's Black Friday and the tax return deadline at the end of January are both opportunities for fraudsters to scam you.

That's because people are more likely to expect emails, texts and other correspondence at this time of year.

Fraudsters will use the dates to impersonate genuine organisations and retailers by pushing victims towards fake websites to steal personal information.

Here's how to spot a scam and how you can avoid being caught out.

If you are self-employed or have more than one source of income you will need to file a tax return by the end of January.

Fraudsters seize on this deadline to blitz people with messages that look like they are official and from the UK's tax authority, HMRC.

Any such calls or messages should be ignored, but you can also check whether an email, external, text message, external or phone call, external claiming to be from HMRC is genuine.

"Reporting scams helps us shut them down and protect others," adds Lucy Pike, HMRC's chief security officer.

New figures from HMRC, shared with the BBC, show that it received 95,340 reports of suspected scams in the year to the end of October.

There were 900 reports of one specific scam which used emails containing a malicious attachment which directed customers to a fake gov.uk webpage designed to steal personal and financial information.

it never contacts people by email, text or phone to tell them they are due a refund or ask them to claim one

it never asks for personal or financial information by email or text message

it never leaves threatening voicemails about legal action or arrest

In the run-up to Black Friday you may be looking around for discounts, but fraudsters will impersonate genuine retailers to steal personal details or take payment for a product that will never be delivered.

Kirsty Adams, a fraud and scams expert at High Street bank Barclays, says make sure you pause before you buy.

"When you're scrolling on the sofa and a countdown timer starts ticking, or a deal pops up in your feed from an account that looks just like a brand you trust, it's easy to get swept up and pay before you've had a chance to check.

"No deal is worth rushing for. So take a moment before you buy."

The bank also advises doing these four things:

Pause and check, rather than being blinded by a countdown timer

Pay safely using a credit or debit card via a trusted payment service, not a bank transfer to a stranger

Open an official website or app yourself rather than clicking on links in emails, ads or social posts

Talk to a friend, family or your bank if you think something does not feel right, and calling your bank straight away if you think you've been scammed

Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

Don't fall for this - how to protect yourself from AI scams

#126. International student enrollment drops sharply, putting more U.S. colleges in financial jeopardy

Amid changing visa policies and rising degree costs, significantly fewer new international students enrolled at U.S. colleges this year — a shift that could have financial repercussions for colleges, students and the broader economy.

In the fall 2026 semester, the tally of new international students studying in the U.S. sank 15% compared to the prior academic year, according to a fall snapshot released Monday from the Institute of International Education, an international education nonprofit.

Over the 2025-26 academic year, the number of new international students in the U.S. fell more than 17%, largely because fewer graduate students came from countries such as India and China, the IIE's report found.

A growing number of U.S. colleges said that international student recruitment was a top concern, but the report also cited the high cost of a U.S. degree relative to other countries — along with uncertainty around new rules ending the traditional "duration of status" system and limiting most F-1 and J-1 visas to four years — as significant barriers.

Even though the Department of Homeland Security's new visa restrictions are currently on hold under a federal court injunction, experts say questions about the changing policies may have already discouraged some prospective international students.

"At a time when countries around the world are competing for talent, the United States should be strengthening our ability to attract the best and brightest, not creating new barriers to it," Fanta Aw, executive director and CEO of NAFSA: Association of International Educators, said in a Sept. 14 statement after a federal court temporarily blocked the Department of Homeland Security's new visa rules.

Foreign students typically pay full tuition, making international enrollment an important revenue source for colleges and universities, according to Robert Franek, editor-in-chief of The Princeton Review.

With fewer tuition dollars coming in, there are fewer resources for U.S. students, particularly for financial aid, he said: "There are going to be fewer research dollars, fewer scholarship dollars for students here in the states."

International students also contributed nearly $40 billion to the U.S. economy during the 2025-26 academic year, according to a separate NAFSA analysis. This year's enrollment decline is expected to cost the economy roughly $2.4 billion.

"The data again reflect the harsh reality that the barriers the U.S. government is putting in place to block talented international students are having a real impact," said NAFSA's Aw. "All Americans will feel the effects as international students' tuition payments, local spending, and contributions to U.S. excellence in research and innovation diminish."

Although the fallout extends well beyond college campuses, other research shows that colleges and the students they serve will bear the brunt of the impact.

Recent changes to F-1 student visa rules could heighten financial risks for some U.S. colleges and universities, particularly those that rely heavily on students from abroad, according to an analysis from Fitch Ratings released in August.

"Sustained drops in new international student enrollment can have outsized revenue effects, as international students often pay full tuition or receive less institutional aid than domestic students," Fitch analysts said in the report. "Lost revenue is hard to replace quickly."

Specifically, universities with large graduate and STEM — science, technology, engineering and math — programs, where time to degree completion can exceed four years, will have a harder time sustaining the enrollment pipeline and "may incur higher costs to address overseas recruitment challenges," the analysts wrote.

Most international students — 57% — study in STEM fields, according to IIE's report, so enrollment declines can have an outsized financial impact on schools with large engineering and computer science programs.

Underscoring the importance of those students, 91% of colleges and universities said recruiting international students in STEM fields is now a key priority, according to IIE's fall snapshot, which was based on a survey of 809 institutions.

#124. Gas starts flowing from Jackdaw after government green light

The Jackdaw platform is located 150 miles east of Aberdeen

Gas has begun flowing from the Jackdaw gas field, with the UK energy secretary saying it will help to heat millions of homes and businesses this winter.

Ministers last month announced the Jackdaw field, which lies 150 miles east of Aberdeen, had been approved after years of uncertainty and a court case.

It has now been announced that it has begun producing gas which will start flowing into Britain, according to its operator Adura.

The UK government approved production from the gas field in the North Sea last month, amid intense debate about the project's environmental and economic impact.

Miliband says he won't resign if government approves Rosebank oil field

Burnham defends Jackdaw decision as Badenoch urges PM to overturn ban on new oil and gas licences

Jackdaw is operated by Adura under a joint venture between Shell and Norwegian state energy firm Equinor.

A decision is yet to be taken on whether production can begin at Rosebank, the UK's largest known untapped oil field, which is located off the coast of Shetland in the North Atlantic.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said: "Last month, I gave Jackdaw the green light and today its gas will start flowing into Britain, thanks to the hard work of the skilled North Sea workers.

"It means homegrown gas from this project will help to heat millions of British homes and businesses this winter and beyond, in a boost for our energy security.

"It also will help minimise the UK's use of imported higher emission gas as we continue our transition to renewables and nuclear."

Claire Mueller, Jackdaw project director at Adura, said the gas field was "now helping to power Britain".

She added: "At a time of considerable uncertainty around the world, Jackdaw will provide secure energy from our own waters to heat British homes and power our economy, while reducing our reliance on imports with higher emissions.

"Jackdaw also demonstrates what can be achieved when Britain makes pragmatic, evidence-led decisions about the energy it needs, while properly accounting for our climate responsibilities."

Mueller said the decision was a "positive step towards restoring confidence in the North Sea".

She added that Adura was "proud to be producing the oil and gas our country will continue to need for decades".

Why is the Jackdaw gas field approval so controversial?

Adura has said Jackdaw will provide about 6% of UK gas production, enough to power 1.4 million homes. After imports are included, that amounts to about 2% of total UK demand.

The gas will be pumped to the existing Shearwater platform for processing â known as a tie-back in industry terms â before being piped to the St Fergus gas terminal in Aberdeenshire.

The UK government said that over its expected 11-year lifespan, Jackdaw would support the UK's gas supply, protect hundreds of jobs and add Â£2.9bn to the economy.

The decision to green-light Jackdaw drew criticism from some Labour backbenchers and environmentalists, while Conservatives called on the government to also approve the Rosebank oil field.

Both projects were approved for development by the previous Tory government in 2022 and 2023 respectively but these decisions were struck down by the Scottish courts after a challenge from environmental campaigners.

This followed a Supreme Court ruling that developers had to account for the climate impact caused by burning fossil fuels in their planning applications.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has pledged to stick to the UK's net zero target despite giving the green light to Jackdaw.

He previously said the decision would "significantly strengthen our energy security by increasing domestic gas production by about 6% and support the UK's energy supply for more than a decade".

A decision on the larger Rosebank oil project is yet to be made.

Protesters gathered outside the Department for Energy Security and Net Zero in Westminster on Wednesday morning

The co-leader of the Scottish Greens, Ross Greer, described the Jackdaw approval as "climate vandalism".

He said: "It is a devastating betrayal of our children's future and it won't even lower energy bills in the here and now."

Environmental campaigners argue that this summer's heatwaves demonstrate the need to tackle climate change by rejecting both Jackdaw and Rosebank.

Areeba Hamid, the co-executive director of Greenpeace, said approving Jackdaw was "the wrong decision" in terms of lowering energy bills, providing energy security, and addressing climate change.

Oxfam said the government had "drilled straight through the UK's climate commitments" and accused the prime minister of sending "the wrong signal as the impacts of climate change intensify".

The charity says the government must commit to not approving any new oil and gas licences, including the proposals for Rosebank.

Jackdaw's operators said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages

Jackdaw sits close to the boundary between the British and Norwegian sectors of the North Sea, in an area where Westminster originally granted licences to explore for oil and gas in 1970 and 1989.

The field was discovered in 2005 but it was expensive and technically difficult to exploit.

Shell did not commit to its development until 2022, shortly after the Conservative government had granted consent for production.

But in January 2025, after a challenge by environmentalists, the Court of Session in Edinburgh ruled that consent for Jackdaw and Rosebank had been granted unlawfully.

The judge, Lord Ericht, said the developers should have provided a more detailed account of the fields' impact on climate change as part of the permitting process.

The judge allowed the companies to continue with preparatory work short of producing oil and gas while they crafted new environmental impact assessments taking into account the eventual effect of actually burning the fossil fuels, not just the emissions which would be created in extracting them.

An inside look at Jackdaw, the UKâs newest and most contested gas platform.

When BBC News visited the platform this summer, Adura CEO Neil McCulloch said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages this winter.

However, the UK government has previously suggested that Jackdaw will have little impact on energy security and none on reducing domestic gas and electricity bills.

In August, in an interim response to a consultation on the future of the country's gas supplies, Energy Minister Michael Shanks said the UK would inevitably become more reliant on imports as domestic production declined, external.

"With diminishing output from the North Sea over the past decades, any gas produced from the UK Continental Shelf represents a tiny fraction of global demand and so will not lower prices nor materially boost supply," wrote Shanks.

Labour has previously said it is opposed to the issuing of new exploration licences but it will consider new production projects if they are tied back to existing facilities.

McCulloch described the Jackdaw decision as "an important and positive step forward" - but said attention now turned to Rosebank.

He has urged the UK government to approve production from the field, in which Adura owns an 80% stake. The other 20% is held by London-based Ithaca Energy.

Rosebank contains an estimated 300 million to 500 million barrels of oil.

But while most of Jackdaw's gas is likely to be consumed in the UK, Rosebank's oil will be taken off by tanker and sold on international markets.

UK government approves drilling in Jackdaw gas field with production to start this month

Chris Mason: Jackdaw approved but tricky energy trade-offs remain

#122. Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Co. that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalice said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalice said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

Correction: This story has been updated to correct the spelling of Alexis Scalice's name.

#120. Social Security and the blue wave: What could happen to payroll taxes and senior tax breaks

Congress has six years to enact changes to Social Security to prevent cuts to retirement benefits. Senators elected in the Nov. 3 midterms will be in office when the deadline arrives, and their decisions may be critical in making tax changes that can affect Social Security.

During the midterm campaign season, tax policy surfaced in the debate over Social Security in two different ways. Many Democrats, and some Republicans, called for high earners to pay more in Social Security payroll taxes, while many Republicans touted a recently enacted tax deduction for adults 65 and older.

The diverging tactics come at a crucial moment for reform: The first could increase revenue flowing into the program. The second reduces eligible older adults' income taxes and can lower the taxes some beneficiaries pay on Social Security benefits, reducing program revenue.

Social Security trustees' latest projections show the trust fund used to pay retirement benefits may start to become depleted in 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable.

Lawmakers do have some leeway — if the program's retirement and disability trust funds are combined, depletion would be pushed out to 2034, when 83% of scheduled benefits would be payable. But that's still a haircut for retirees — and it's coming soon.

"We've waited too long already, and the consequences are that the gap has grown larger," Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said of Social Security's shortfall.

On the campaign trail, midterm candidates largely avoided specifics on how they would reform Social Security. However, raising the payroll tax cap, also known as the taxable maximum, was a popular choice among Democratic candidates. That would require people who earn more than the cap, currently $184,500, to begin contributing payroll taxes for their earnings above that amount.

Meanwhile, some Republican candidates said they would extend the temporary senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per eligible individual that is set to expire in 2028.

The deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act passed in July of last year and billed as a senior bonus, was among the factors that moved Social Security trustees' retirement trust fund depletion projections three months sooner, according to an annual report released in June.

Here's what experts say could be in store now that Democrats have won control of the House and Senate.

Social Security is a pay-as-you-go system that relies on payroll taxes from workers to help make monthly benefit payments to millions of Americans.

In 2026, earnings up to $184,500 are subject to that tax. Workers and employers each contribute 6.2% of pay up to that threshold, while self-employed individuals pay 12.4%.

The wage cap will rise in 2027 to $192,300. It adjusts higher annually to reflect incremental changes in the average wage index.

On the campaign trail, some House and Senate candidates said the cap should be raised or eliminated entirely so high earners contribute more.

Following the election, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., who is expected to become the next chair of the Social Security subcommittee, said on X on Nov. 7 that his proposal to increase benefits by at least $2,400 per year and "scrap the cap" on earnings subject to Social Security taxes would be a "top priority."

Outgoing Sen. Bill Cassidy, R-La., responded on X that the plan would "put a 12.4% tax" on people who are not millionaires or billionaires.

The idea of higher payroll taxes has broader support. A national survey fielded in February found most respondents — 79% — support raising taxes for wages over $400,000, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. The change was the most popular option tested among the 2,509 adults surveyed.

Rising income inequality has meant the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings, though that percentage was expected to remain constant when lawmakers enacted the last major reforms to the program in 1983, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized at that level, according to the institute.

Despite candidates mentioning plans to change the cap during election season, some experts say Congress may put off tax hikes that could be politically unpopular.

"I think lawmakers may put some of these taxes on the table closer to 2032, when the Social Security trust fund hits insolvency," said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. "In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute."

If the payroll tax cap were lifted from just under $200,000 to $300,000 in earnings per year, that would cost someone at that higher income threshold about $6,700 more in direct payroll taxes, Riedl said.

"There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term," Riedl said. In the long term, though, "everything is on the table," she said.

While many Democrats have pushed for raising those taxes, some Republicans have also shared their openness to doing so.

"There's a crack in the dam there breaking," Dan Adcock, director of government relations and policy at the National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, said of the Republican support.

Even if lawmakers raise or eliminate the cap, they would still need to agree on other changes to shore up the program. Republicans may want benefit cuts such as raising the retirement age in exchange for increasing taxes, Adcock said.

Just how far adjusting the taxable maximum would go to help solve Social Security's funding woes depends on how it is applied.

If the cap is eliminated starting in 2027, with the full 12.4% tax applying to all earnings and no benefit credit for earnings above the current threshold, that would eliminate 58% of the long-range actuarial balance over 75 years, according to a September analysis by the Social Security Administration.

Applying the 12.4% payroll tax rate to earnings over $250,000 starting in 2027 could address 57% of the long-range balance, according to the SSA, provided there are no benefit increases in exchange for the extra contributions.

If the 12.4% payroll tax rate were applied to earnings over $400,000 starting in 2027 without benefit increases, that would eliminate 53% of the long-range actuarial balance, the agency's analysis found.

Eventually, all earnings would be subject to Social Security payroll taxes as the gap between the taxable maximum, which increases every year, and the new threshold closes over time.

Yet some experts say raising the payroll tax cap could have consequences for taxpayers and the economy.

Raising the cap would be an "unprecedented increase" in marginal tax rates, according to William McBride, chief economist at the Tax Foundation, an independent tax policy research organization.

Lifting the threshold could add more than 12 percentage points to the cost of earning an additional dollar of wages, McBride said. Those higher costs could affect the labor market, which may see higher unemployment as a result of the change, he said.

OBBBA's new senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per individual — or "bonus additional amount for seniors," as it was dubbed in an early version of the legislation — is in effect for tax years 2025 through 2028.

Lawmakers elected to Congress in the midterms may decide whether to extend the temporary tax break.

"2028 is going to be a presidential election year, and I would not expect either party to be willing to allow any senior tax cuts to expire while they're competing for senior votes," Riedl said.

When President Donald Trump signed OBBBA into law in 2025, the White House said in a release: "No tax on Social Security is a reality." However, the new law does not eliminate taxes on Social Security benefits.

Instead, it provides a deduction that reduces seniors' taxable income regardless of whether they have claimed Social Security benefits.

During the campaign, Republicans touted the $6,000 senior deduction as tax relief and criticized Democrats for voting against the law that enacted it. However, congressional Democrats unanimously opposed OBBBA, citing provisions including cuts to Medicaid and food assistance.

Some effects of the new $6,000 senior deduction are different from those of eliminating taxes on benefits, according to McBride at the Tax Foundation. The senior deduction affects lower- and middle-income taxpayers to a greater degree, while eliminating taxes on Social Security benefits would be more beneficial to upper-income taxpayers, McBride said.

How Social Security benefits are currently taxed

Individuals with $25,000 to $34,000 in combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest and half of annual Social Security benefits — pay federal taxes on up to 50% of their Social Security benefits. For individuals with over $34,000 in combined income, up to 85% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax.

For married couples filing jointly with between $32,000 and $44,000 in combined income, up to 50% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax. For married couples with more than $44,000 in combined income, that increases to up to 85% of their benefits.

The senior bonus tax deduction allows those age 65 and older to deduct up to $6,000 for individuals, or up to $12,000 for married couples. The deduction phases out for modified adjusted gross income over $75,000 for single filers and $150,000 for married couples.

The senior bonus deduction — together with the tax year 2025 standard deduction of $15,750 for single taxpayers and $31,500 for married couples and the existing senior deduction for those who qualify of $2,000 for singles and $3,200 for married couples — "amounts to the largest tax break in history for America's seniors," the White House said in July 2025. In 2026, the standard deduction is $16,100 for single filers and $32,200 for married couples.

Under OBBBA, 88% of beneficiaries, or 51.4 million individuals age 65 and older receiving Social Security income, will have exemptions and deductions that exceed their taxable Social Security income, according to the administration. Prior to the law, 64% of beneficiaries, or 37.2 million individuals, had tax breaks that exceeded their taxable Social Security income.

But the measure does nothing to resolve Social Security's depletion problem.

In an annual report released in June, Social Security's trustees cited OBBBA as having a "substantial effect" on the program's financial status because beneficiaries will pay less in income taxes on benefits. In that report, the trustees moved the projected depletion date for the program's retirement trust fund to the fourth quarter of 2032 — up one quarter from the previous year's projections.

"This new deduction for seniors cuts into some of the funding for the trust fund, so it makes the solvency date a little nearer in time," McBride said. "It makes the program a little more fragile, if you will."

#119. UK will have 'voice' but no vote on EU food rules in Brexit reset

The UK will have a "voice in the room" but no vote on European Union food policies as part of a so-called "Brexit reset" to reduce red tape, EU relations minister Hamish Falconer has told the BBC.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham hopes to agree a deal at a summit on Friday but critics have said a reset will make the UK "a rule taker" but not a rule maker.

Falconer said the UK will be involved in a process to shape the rules and said the deal could help bring down prices for shoppers.

The reset would not involve any upfront payments, Falconer added, but made it clear there could well be an "exit clause" in case a future UK government reversed the closer alignment.

Falconer said it was "reasonable" to consider what would happen if a future Conservative or Reform government wanted to undo the new arrangements.

"The EU is worried about that and you can see why," he said.

The BBC understands that the clause is a result of EU concerns about the possible cost of having to rebuild facilities for border food safety checks which were put in place following Brexit.

In September, the Conservative Party said it was "raising the alarm" over the government's planned reset, saying it would leave British consumers and businesses "dangerously exposed, with no input on how regulations are made or power to reject them".

Both the Conservatives and Reform have said they would undo the planned reset if they were in power.

Minister for EU relations Hamish Falconer

The prime minister has said he believes there is a "win-win" deal to be had with the EU.

Speaking at a Marks & Spencer food court in central London, Falconer said the deal would help bring down costs for firms importing food.

"This will mean less cost for supermarkets, and I hope that less cost then turns into cheaper produce on the shelves," he said.

He said currently "pages and pages" of form-filling meant some suppliers had given up exporting to the EU and some products, such as Lincolnshire sausages, meant they were almost impossible to export at all.

Some M&S trucks require over 300 pages of documents for food exports, from export health certificates, phytosanitary certificates, visits from vets and border check paperwork.

Some exports are rejected for having the wrong colour of ink on forms, or an inaccurate Latin name for cooked meats.

"The amount of paperwork you would need to do, the amount of uncertainty - you have to have it in the right pen colour, if it's not black, it might be refused," Falconer said.

"If you're selling Cox apples from Kent, they have to wait a day before they go into the European Union."

The reset could also mean "tearing up" checks that were introduced on goods flowing from Great Britain to Northern Ireland, following Brexit, he said, as well as separate "Not for EU" labelling.

Falconer said the impact of the reset deal for food would be an additional Â£5bn.

The whole deal to be signed on Friday is calculated to add 0.4% to UK GDP.

This wider agreement includes aligning emissions trading rules, and agreeing to a youth mobility scheme, as well as the reset on food trading.

Some British sausages have faced barriers to being exported to the EU

Tom Bradshaw, president of the National Farmers' Union, welcomed reduced border inspections and paperwork but said that for the NFU it was "a moment of opportunity and risk" with farmers waiting to see what carve-outs and transitional arrangements would be included.

Trade volumes in food exports to the EU had "fallen significantly - by over a third" since 2019, he said.

But the UK should take a "pragmatic" approach to implementing any new rules.

He raised pesticides and precision breeding - or gene edited crops - as areas where he hoped some compromise would be found.

UK firms are, for example, developing tomatoes engineered to contain extra vitamin D.

Historically the EU has had much stricter rules on gene-editing, but this year relaxed regulations.

Falconer said people would have to "wait and see the detail" in this area.

He said there were bound to be compromises and trade-offs in any deal but that where the UK had advantages the government wanted to make sure "those aren't lost under the deal".

Another contentious area has been crisp flavourings, with concerns raised that stricter EU rules on certain ingredients might limit the sale of some popular snack flavours in the UK.

"I want to take this opportunity to absolutely stamp down on misinformation about smoky bacon crisps," Falconer said.

Post-Brexit the UK had stayed "pretty closely aligned" to European food safety standards, he said.

Two leading snack brands had already adapted their recipes to accommodate EU rules, an example of the kind of alignment that would happen whether or not there was a reset of UK-EU relations.

"The truth is, almost every business in this country that wants to export food or drink will be aligning with EU rules because it's our biggest market.

"Lots of people, like the good people at Walkers and Frazzles have made these changes already, so I would want to reassure people, particularly smoky bacon fans, it's okay," he said.

Could smoky bacon crisps really be banned?

Ten years on, Brexit's economic impact is becoming clearer

#117. ‘Standing on one leg’: IMF chief warns about global economy as AI circular financing hits $1 trillion

The global economy is "on the AI leg," International Monetary Fund Managing Director Kristalina Georgieva said Monday, warning that the overdependence on the sector risks the world's financial stability.

Georgieva flagged that a "sudden drop in investment confidence and withdrawal from the AI field" could weaken economic growth, speaking with CNBC's JP Ong on the sidelines of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations' summit in the Philippines.

"We have a world economy that is standing on one leg on the AI leg. If this leg wobbles, that can lead to financial stability troubles," Georgieva warned.

Circular financing among AI companies — where money invested in an AI company ultimately flows back to the investor through purchase orders — has grown to a little over $1 trillion, two-thirds of it funded by debt, the IMF estimates. "It's not a huge size, but it is a rapid increase," Georgieva said.

Nvidia Corp, which has seen more than $1 trillion wiped out of its market value over the past month, is at the center of these circular financial deals. The fallout from the chipmaker's former CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy, has led to a sell-off in global AI-linked stocks and put the spotlight on the company's role as a key financier of the AI infrastructure buildout.

UBS analyst Tim Arcuri estimated in a recent note that an Nvidia financing pullback could easily remove $50 billion or more out of the system, at a time when rising interest rates have already made corporate borrowing more expensive.

Georgieva's warning comes as the AI build-out has been a critical engine of growth for the global economy, propping up trade and investment.

The IMF raised its 2026 global growth forecast to 3.1% in October as major economies lean on AI investment to power growth otherwise curtailed by the energy crunch caused by the Iran war.

The institution estimated that about 40% of the world's workforce will be affected by AI. Capital Economics suggested that as much as 80% of the recent expansion in global exports was concentrated in AI-related goods.

Investors' frenzy for AI reflects expectations running ahead of what the technology has proven to deliver, Georgieva said. "We tend to rush ahead," as valuations climb, she said, therefore "some corrections" become necessary.

IMF in October had warned that a short-term disappointment could set off a disorderly correction across asset classes: in its worst case scenario, if AI fails to live up to the market's lofty expectations, U.S. equity prices could fall 20% in 2026 and global output end up 1.1% below its baseline estimate by 2028.

Global equites, including U.S. chipmakers, have been seeing a bruising sell-off over the past few weeks, with investor appetite for the AI trade facing a fresh test this week ahead of earnings from Nvidia and Anthropic's blockbuster listing.

Georgieva on Monday characterized the recent market swings as "orderly," and unlikely to spiral into anything that could undermine the world economy, while urging caution. "We have to be watchful."

#116. Intel sued by investors after PC sales slump hits share price

Intel, the US chip giant, is being sued by its shareholders who accuse the firm of hiding the severity of a slump in its personal computer (PC) sales that caused its share price to plunge almost 29% in one day.

Billions of dollars was knocked off the value of the company after it forecast disappointing revenues ahead of its latest earnings report.

Shareholders allege that Intel made "materially false and misleading" statements that failed to disclose the slowdown in PC sales and the impact of its data centre projects, according to a proposed class action lawsuit filed in San Jose, California.

In October, Intel firm announced its computer hardware sales had dropped sharply in the three months that ended in September.

That report undermined earlier suggestions from the chipmaker that demand had been improving, sending Intel shares plunging, according to the lawsuit.

Shareholders claimed the "depressed demand for PC sales" caused "a dramatic slowdown" in quarterly earnings â a drop of 9%, or about $800m - to $8.1bn.

The lawsuit, filed by legal firm Robbins Geller Rudman & Dowd on behalf of owners of Intel common shares between 23 July and 29 October this year, was filed against the chipmaker, its chief executive Tan Lip-Bu and other company leaders.

Intel has poured huge investments into manufacturing to meet the enormous demand for AI computing power.

As more semiconductors are channelled towards data centres, chips for PCs and other electronics have become more expensive.

Intel boss Tan had told analysts that the industry was facing an "unprecedented memory crunch", resulting in a shortage of chip supplies for PCs.

"We need to address these challenges with urgency," Tan said in October.

Intel shares have fallen sharply in the past month by 37.5%, though its share price is still up by 51% overall this year.

#114. Bitcoin slides to $62,000 following liquidation cascade, then rebounds slightly

Bitcoin fell to its lowest levels since August over the weekend as a wave of forced liquidations accelerated amid broader weakening in market liquidity and risk appetite.

The price of the flagship cryptocurrency fell as low as $62,000 late Saturday, according to Coin Metrics, and is coming off its sixth weekly decline in a row. On Monday morning, it had climbed back to the $64,000 level.

Bitcoin's ongoing weakness pushed its price below key technical support levels during the week, breaking below its 200-day moving average of about $73,000 and triggering the cascade of long liquidations, forcing traders to sell their assets at market price to settle their debts, pushing prices lower.

On Saturday night, exchanges tracked by CoinGlass saw more than $165 million in liquidated bitcoin long positions, following $629 million on Friday and nearly $700 million on Thursday.

Bitcoin was also facing potential selling pressure ahead of Anthropic's anticipated initial public offering, with the listing drawing speculative capital away from riskier assets.

On Friday, the dollar index – which has an inverse relationship with bitcoin – reached its highest level since April 2025, following hotter-than-expected inflation earlier this week that increased expectations of further Fed tightening. That pushed Treasury yields and the dollar higher while pressuring both bitcoin and gold, despite their appeal as alternative stores of value in the longer term.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from the combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen.

"A break below $60,000 is possible if Nvidia's results add to the tech selling," he said. "The catalysts for a move higher are mostly macro, and a softer dollar with falling real yields would ease the pressure on every risk asset at once. If that happens, bitcoin goes into the recovery with less leverage than it carried in October, and a close back above $73,000 would be the first sign that the selling is over."

Edwin Mata, CEO of tokenization platform Brickken, said he's monitoring whether spot buyers step in once the forced selling subsides.

"Clearing out leveraged longs can slow the selling," he said. "For a recovery to hold, spot buyers need to keep showing up after the forced selling has passed."

#113. Create more jobs for under-25s by cutting National Insurance, key report urges

Salon owner: 'We have parents offering to pay their kids' wages'

National Insurance (NI) paid by firms on under-25s should be gradually lowered to help create more jobs, a major report will urge.

There are nearly one million 16-24 year-olds not in employment, education or training (Neets) in the UK, official figures show.

Reducing NI costs for firms starting with retail and hospitality firms - which provide almost 40% of jobs for these people - will help improve their opportunities, the report will suggest.

Collette Osborne, a hairdresser who owns two salons in Nottingham, said she feels "suffocated by employment costs" including NI and while she used to employ eight apprentices a year, now she cannot afford to take on any.

Alan Milburn's report will also make recommendations on cutting the benefits bill

Her workforce has more than halved to 21 staff. Osborne told the BBC it costs "around Â£60,000 to train an apprentice before we can actually start allowing them onto customers' heads".

She said she has had parents so desperate for their children to find employment at her salons that some have even offered to pay their wages.

"I'm inundated with applications - and I mean inundated - which is heartbreaking because I can't take them on," she said.

Osborne does not feel she is getting help to offer jobs to youngsters.

"I feel like the government's foot is firmly on my neck," she said. "They promise breathing space. I feel suffocated, suffocated by employment costs."

The independent report by former Labour minister Alan Milburn has been in the works for a year and will be published on Tuesday in full.

He has already unveiled some of his proposals including:

Reducing benefits - under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide Universal Credit or personal independence payment (Pip), which is the main disability benefit in England and Wales

A legal guarantee of a job, training or employment support with those who refuse to take up potentially losing their benefits.

Doubling the threshold at which businesses start paying NI for 21 to 24-year-olds from Â£5,000 to Â£10,000.

At the moment, businesses pay no employer NI contributions for workers younger than 21 or for apprentices under 25 â unless they earn above a threshold of Â£50,270.

However, for non-apprentices aged 21-24, employers pay 15% on annual earnings above Â£5,000.

This has been the situation since April 2025, after the then Chancellor Rachel Reeves increased the rate from 13.8% and lowered the threshold from Â£9,100.

Under Sir Keir Starmer, the government raised NI for firms but Milburn will say the Neets issue was not started by the increase.

Immediately axing NI for 21 to 24-year-olds would cost Â£5.1bn, according to research by the Resolution Foundation, a left-leaning think tank.

Milburn is set to say that an immediate across-the-board cut in employer NI is not the best use of several billions of pounds of public money.

But shadow work and pensions secretary, Helen Whately, said the government should "just stop doing the taxing".

Milburn told the BBC's Laura Kuenssberg programme he supported the government subsidising wages to encourage businesses to take on young workers.

"Because young people today are walking into the workplace often with a mental health problem, ADHD or anxiety, if you're a small business that's a cost that you've got to absorb," he said.

"The state has a responsibility to help them with that."

On Sunday, Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden said the government would extend its subsidised jobs guarantee scheme for 18 to 24-year-olds who are claiming Universal Credit.

People who have been on benefits for 12 months will be eligible, down from 18 months and the scheme would be available for people in England, Scotland and Wales.

McFadden said the scheme, which guarantees workers a six-month job, "will help more than 90,000 young people into work by March 2029".

Additional reporting by Ollie Smith and Anuk Weerawardana

#111. A major NFT marketplace is shutting down—5 years after the boom, there's a lesson for investors

Five years ago, NFTs seemed to be everywhere.

Although the digital collectibles, known as nonfungible tokens, had existed since 2014, they exploded in popularity in 2021 alongside the cryptocurrency boom, becoming status symbols for celebrities and wealthy collectors.

Weekly NFT trading volume across major blockchains surged from about $4.4 million in late 2020 to a peak of $3.2 billion in August 2021, according to CryptoSlam data published by The Block.

Today, much of that trading activity has disappeared. Weekly NFT trading volume has fallen more than 99% since August 2021, and several major NFT marketplaces have closed. On Thursday, Blur, once the largest marketplace for Ethereum-based NFTs, shut down its website.

At the height of the boom, Twitter co-founder Jack Dorsey sold an NFT of his first tweet for $2.9 million, while a digital artwork by an artist known as Beeple fetched $69.3 million at Christie's. Paris Hilton and Jimmy Fallon showed off their Bored Ape NFTs on "The Tonight Show," and even McDonald's released NFTs celebrating the return of its McRib sandwich.

Back then, some buyers believed NFTs represented the future of digital ownership, while others bet they could resell them at higher prices. But as enthusiasm faded, so did the demand that had driven the digital tokens' values higher.

The NFT boom and bust shows how quickly speculative investments can unravel when demand dries up. For investors, it's a cautionary tale about spotting the warning signs before buying into the next craze, financial experts tell CNBC Make It.

Part of the initial appeal of NFTs was the promise of a new way for artists and other creators to make money from their work. Using blockchain technology, NFTs allowed creators to sell digital collectibles with verifiable ownership records and potentially earn royalties on subsequent sales through smart contracts.

More broadly, NFTs were touted as part of the future of digital ownership, with the potential to transform how people bought, sold and traded digital assets.

But establishing ownership of a digital collectible was different from creating lasting demand for it, says Evan Mills, a financial advisor at wealth management firm Scholar Advising.

"NFTs tried to manufacture that demand," he says. "Blockchain could establish who owns the token, but it couldn't guarantee that anyone would really value the ownership at the end of the day."

In practice, many of the NFTs that attracted attention were relatively simple digital images, such as cartoon animals or promotional artwork featuring beer cans.

Traditional collectibles, such as paintings, antiques and baseball cards, have established histories and communities of collectors that can sustain demand, Mills says. NFTs, by comparison, became enormously popular in a relatively short period.

Unlike many traditional collectibles, however, NFTs could be created in virtually unlimited quantities. While individual collections might contain a limited number of tokens, there was little stopping creators from launching new series. At the height of the boom in 2022, roughly 500 new NFT collections were being created every day, according to blockchain analytics firm IntoTheBlock.

The rapid rise in NFT prices helped fuel expectations that buyers would continue paying more for them. But as enthusiasm faded, so did the pool of potential buyers.

And unlike stocks or bonds, most NFTs didn't generate income, meaning buyers hoping to turn a profit generally depended on someone else being willing to pay more for them, says Martin Mulyadi, the YHB Endowed Chair in Accounting at Shenandoah University.

"Most NFTs paid nothing, so the only way to make money was finding a next buyer at a higher price," he says. "When the buyers stopped coming, there was nothing holding up the price."

For everyday investors, the NFT bust is a reminder that an asset's popularity doesn't necessarily make it a worthwhile investment.

One common justification for buying risky investments like collectibles is that they make up only a small portion of an otherwise diversified portfolio. Financial advisors often recommend limiting speculative holdings to money an investor can afford to lose. But keeping an investment small doesn't necessarily make it worthwhile, Mulyadi says.

"While keeping a bet small limits the damage, it doesn't turn a weak asset into a good one," he says.

Mills argues that investors can also confuse speculation with diversification.

"Diversification is not just owning more things," he says. "It's owning things that have different economic characteristics that change the risk and return of your overall portfolio."

For collectibles, that means looking beyond an item's scarcity or popularity to whether there's an established market of buyers likely to value it over time. Even then, future demand can be difficult to predict.

There's also a difference between buying a collectible because you actually like it and buying it because you think someone else will pay more for it. For many NFT speculators, the appeal was likely less about owning what was essentially a JPEG than the prospect of reselling it for a profit.

Before buying a speculative asset, Mills recommends asking two questions: What is the economic reason it should generate a return, and what role does it play in the overall portfolio? If an investor can't answer those questions, he says, the investment may not belong in the portfolio at all.

"A bad investment doesn't become a good investment just because you only own a little bit of it," Mills says.

#110. Elon Musk joins 'Bring Jensen Back' campaign to reinstate the ex-Nvidia boss

SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Nvidia's former chief executive Jensen Huang

Allies of Jensen Huang are trying to rally support for the former Nvidia boss to get his job back after resigning last week over a scandal involving extramarital affairs, one involving an alleged Chinese spy.

SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, chief executive of Fortnite-maker Epic Games, are among those backing a campaign to get the AI chip giant's board to "Bring Jensen Back".

Huang resigned as chief executive of Nvidia after the emergence of two affairs, one of whom was with a woman suspected of spying for China and another allegedly with one of his employees.

It has created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms which is a key part of the AI industry and its huge expansion.

An open letter calling for Nvidia's board to give Huang his job back while an internal probe is conducted has received more than 175,000 signatures, including Musk and Sweeney, according to a website named "Stand with Jensen".

The campaign group said it is made up of current and former Nvidia employees, partners, shareholders and supporters.

"We write with gratitude for Jensen Huang and with a clear demand to the board: bring Jensen back while an independent investigation establishes the facts, unless and until you can provide compelling evidence that his removal is in NVIDIA's best interests," the letter said.

Allies of the ex-Nvidia boss wrote they were "concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process".

Huang had been expected to weather the storm following the revelation of an affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy.

However, he resigned last Thursday after a second extra-marital relationship emerged, this time allegedly with an executive at Nvidia who reported to him.

The scandal-hit tech boss is now discussing a possible return to Nvidia with confidants, according to the Financial Times., external

The newspaper quoted one ally saying that a "war room" of Silicon Valley executives was being convened to lobby for the former Nvidia boss's reinstatement.

A representative for Jensen Huang declined to comment on the report.

The so-called war room also wants to get support for Huang from the White House.

While President Donald Trump recently spoke in glowing terms about Huang, the scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end Nvidia chips to Alice Huang's former firm, Megaspeed.

It has raised questions about the possibility that the chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The former Nvidia boss has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China.

The BBC understands that the likelihood of Huang being reinstated is low while the Nvidia's internal investigations are ongoing.

The FBI is also investigating the affair between the former Nvidia boss and Alice Huang, according to Trump.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation.

"Jensen drove supply chain brilliantly at Nvidia but many of those deals were a handshake with Jensen, not a written contract.

"Without Jensen, at least some of those critical deals will be lost," he posted on X., external

He later added: "If Jensen gave me his word about compute delivery or I asked for components to be accelerated, he always came through."

If Huang was reinstated, it would face scrutiny from Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has called for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

Shareholders in Nvidia also want Huang back at Nvidia's helm.

In an open letter to the board, Brad Gerstner, founder of Altimeter Capital and Atreides Management boss Gavin Baker called for management to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang".

Lord Sarfraz, a member of the UK's House of Lords, has also written to Nvidia's new chair, Harvey Jones, urging the firm's directors to give "serious consideration" to Huang's return following the investigation.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday.

#108. China confirms Xi-Trump-Putin historic summit. What will be on the agenda?

China confirmed Monday that President Xi Jinping will meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin and U.S. President Donald Trump in Shenzhen on Thursday, setting up the first-ever gathering of the three leaders.

That confirmation from a spokesperson at China's Foreign Ministry follows announcements from Moscow and the White House. The Wall Street Journal had reported last month that Putin had proposed a three-way sit-down in an attempt to negotiate an end to the four-year war in Ukraine.

While the summit among the three nations, on the sidelines of the APEC meeting in Shenzhen, is unprecedented, the leaders have held bilateral meetings over the years. Xi has met with Trump three times since October last year and more than 40 times with Putin since becoming president in 2013. Trump and Putin have met in person on several occasions, including a visit to Alaska by the Russian leader in August 2025.

"For Xi, just the ability to bring Putin and Trump to the table, a first among equals scenario, will deliver much of the political benefit even before the discussions begin," said Peter Alexander, Shanghai-based managing director at Z-Ben Advisors.

Trump has departed Washington, D.C. for a four-day trip to Asia that also includes a stop in the Philippines.

The main hurdle to the first-of-its-kind summit now appears to be "a disagreement over the agenda," said Neo Wang, chief China strategist at Evercore ISI.

The White House signaled the U.S. intention to prioritize talks over nuclear non-proliferation and the Middle East war, while downplaying the prospects of engaging in talks over Ukraine — a topic high on Moscow's agenda.

"He [Trump] will discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world, whose proliferation President Trump believes is very dangerous," Olivia Wales, a White House spokeswoman, told reporters on a briefing call. The president will raise the U.S.-Israeli war against Iran in the meeting but is not expected to bring up Russia's war in Ukraine, the official said.

China has no interest in engaging in nuclear disarmament talks until it achieves parity with the U.S. in about 10 years, said David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy.

Putin's position on the war appears unwavering, with the Russian president telling U.S. envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff in a meeting in Moscow last week that Europe should "figure out" whether "they want peace or war." The meeting came a day after an earlier round of talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine wrapped up in Abu Dhabi.

Russian presidential aide Yuri Ushakov said last week the talks in Shenzhen would continue the diplomatic efforts aimed at reaching a negotiated settlement to the Ukraine war, according to state-controlled local media Russia Today.

The summit will likely yield little concrete result, but presents an "almost 100% photo op" that lifts China's status and power at the top table, said Roche, while Trump's political standing slides. Nvidia and the chip-smuggling issues involving a suspected Chinese spy will stay off the agenda, as Trump steers clear of thorny issues where he is unlikely to claim success.

China and Russia could turn more aggressive with their military ambitions over the next two years if they perceive Trump as politically weakened, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC earlier this month, following the dramatic defeat of Trump's Republican Party in the midterm election.

China did not specify what will be discussed at the three leaders' meeting in Monday's statement.

"Xi may be inclined to do Putin a favor by showing up, given close strategic ties," said Wang. But a joint statement on Ukraine territory, security arrangements or sanctions against Russia appears unlikely, he said, as it would leave Beijing "more exposed to political scrutiny and accountability."

A U.S.-China trade truce set to expire in January is likely to be on the table, with analysts largely expecting an extension despite signs of growing friction in the bilateral relationship.

Aside from an immediate extension, leaders may also look to assess "whether a more durable framework for a rebalancing of U.S.–China trade relations can be established," said Carlos Casanova, senior economist for Asia at UBP.

U.S. trade deficit with China remains substantial, with Beijing's exports to Washington recording double-digit growth for most of this year, from last year's low base. Narrowing that deficit will be central to Washington's call for a more balanced trade, Casanova said.

#106. Retailers are turning to entertainment to win shoppers’ attention

Legacy retailer Gap Inc. made an unconventional hire earlier this year: a chief entertainment officer.

It was a newly created role as the company tapped former Paramount executive Pam Kaufman to helm its entertainment, content and licensing platform. Gap has since rolled out major campaigns, partnering with bands like Just Your Type and building on the success of last year's ads with girl group Katseye.

"Beloved brands have value beyond the products they sell. Consumers identify with them, trust them and want to be part of their world," Kaufman told CNBC in a statement. "The key is authenticity: Every extension has to reflect what the brand stands for and give its fans a meaningful new way to experience it."

She said it's part of a broader goal to emphasize what Gap calls "fashiontainment," a strategy to blend retail and entertainment to better align with consumers. Gap is part of a growing cohort of retailers investing in entertainment and experiences both in-store and through exclusive online content to drive engagement.

"Brands are stories, and we are the storytellers," CEO Richard Dickson said at the Goldman Sachs Global Consumer and Retail conference in September. "I think you could see that come to life when you look at some of the work that we've done, putting our brands in the center of culture and cultural conversation."

In another blending of retail and media, Mattel made waves a few years ago for its "Barbie" movie, building on the intellectual property of a decades-old fan favorite and drawing in massive box office success. At the time, Dickson was president and chief operating officer of the company, witnessing firsthand the sectors' intersection before departing for Gap.

"I think the post-Covid consumer attitude around shopping certainly evolved a lot," said Bryan Eshelman, who leads the retail practice at consulting firm AlixPartners. "It has gone from shopping as a fulfillment task to shopping, in many instances, more as an entertainment form."

Between experiences in physical stores and media campaigns featuring exclusive content, Eshelman said, retailers are getting more creative about the ways they're driving engagement and marketing.

"Community, at the end of the day, is one of the key ways to drive loyalty, so I think retailers of all types are trying to tap into some sense of community, some sense of lifestyle, some sense of belonging that's beyond the product that they offer," he said.

Branded entertainment has been around for a long time. Procter & Gamble made headlines in the 1930s for helping to coin the term "soap opera," and companies have for decades created media campaigns to draw in customers.

But with the rise of social media, content creation and short-form offerings, experts said audiences are becoming hungrier than ever for something new and original.

The retail and entertainment intersection is happening at a time when intellectual property is becoming more valuable than ever, according to experts.

As consumers encounter more marketing than ever, the companies that will win the battle for attention are the ones that can "cut through all of the noise," said Alex Michael, senior managing director at investment firm LionTree.

"The hardest thing to find and to get is audience these days," Michael told CNBC. "IP, in that sense, is the most valuable thing out there, and only becoming more valuable because it's attached to audience."

At the same time, he said, the way IP is distributed is broadening to include video, audio, short-form, long-form and live event content. Consumers are also redefining their loyalty to brands, which means companies have to "play much earlier in the consumer journey" than they have in years past to secure their trust, he said.

The trend is also likely helped by executives who have worked in both retail and entertainment: Gap's Kaufman came from Paramount, for example, and Dickson and Skydance co-CEO Ynon Kreiz held top roles at Mattel.

"The gray areas have never been more pronounced as to what is classically defined as retail or media or entertainment or live events," Michael said. "People who have cross-sector expertise are increasingly more valuable because you can't be defined by a single vertical, especially as things change so quickly."

Still, there's room for newer players, especially with the studio landscape rapidly changing, said Sean Akaks, the CEO of SonderCo, an agency that partners brands with celebrities and entertainment properties.

"I think it's a really interesting time for nontraditional players to be making content because you're seeing this massive consolidation of these legacy entertainment companies," he said. "I think there are just more open lanes for independent creators and also brands and anyone outside the traditional studio system to create content."

That's where retailers might tap creators, Akaks said, because they can use their platforms to reach consumers directly.

"I do think there's a world where we're going to see creators and brands sort of co-creating IP that maybe they're owning together," he said.

Once those companies get consumers into the store, they're also leaning into experiential retail, making shopping a more immersive experience than it otherwise would be, like Dick's Sporting Goods offering interactive activities and simulators in its stores.

According to data from AlixPartners, companies investing in experiential retail such as Coach and Ralph Lauren saw close to 10% year-over-year increases in consumers' average basket size.

"The idea that retailers would potentially invest in some distributed electronic content to help drive traffic, and then when you get there, it's experiential — it really goes into the kinds of flywheels that entertainment companies are also trying to build and grow," said Jeff Goldstein, co-lead of the media and entertainment practice at AlixPartners.

Yet, the entertainment that retail brands are developing is only one piece in a larger puzzle to retain customers and attract new ones. Translating that into tangible sales growth isn't necessarily a given.

"The basics of having great product in stock at a transparent price that feels like value is not swept away by animatronics," AlixPartners' Eshelman said. "The fundamentals are still there, and if you're going to have a good retail proposition, you need to have that balance. You can't mask bad product at a bad price with no inventory with glitz and glamour."

#105. Anthropic’s IPO lands as the embattled AI trade looks for a much-needed bright spot

Between rising interest rates, a nationwide AI backlash, a major political shift in Washington, D.C., and the sudden ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the tech industry is under mounting strain.

Now comes the most important tech IPO in years, and a defining event for the burgeoning but still young artificial intelligence market.

With Anthropic expected to debut on the Nasdaq on Thursday, the stakes are sky-high for almost every major tech company. Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company are collectively investing $26 billion at the IPO price, which is currently expected at $70 to $78 a share. Some of those companies are existing investors, as is early backer Amazon, whose stake could be in the hundreds of billions of dollars.

In addition to all the cash on the line, tech giants also need Anthropic's spending for many years to come. The Claude creator and its top rival, OpenAI, are driving an outsized amount of the AI buildout, and are burning historic amounts of money in the process.

Anthropic has outstanding spending commitments of over $900 billion, according to its latest prospectus. It's on the hook to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon over the course of a decade, and another $400 billion with other companies for cloud capacity, according to filings and partner disclosures. The lab also made $300 billion in commitments with data center and chip partners. Anthropic intends to lay out $125 billion to $150 billion for AI chips from Google, though delivery won't start until 2028.

"The numbers are just staggering," Joe Fath, a partner at venture firm Eclipse and former T. Rowe Price portfolio manager, said in an interview. "It's hard to get your head wrapped around it."

Cloud partners Amazon, Google and Microsoft account for 31% of contractual obligations, Anthropic said in its prospectus. Last year, Amazon built an Indiana data center just for Anthropic, using custom chips.

By going public now, Anthropic can raise a large portion of the money it needs to pay for the technology and infrastructure required for building and hosting its frontier models and expanding portfolio of services. The company recorded a net loss of $104 billion through the first nine months of 2026. The IPO could yield over $90 billion in proceeds, on top of Anthropic's $132 billion in existing cash, including short-term investments.

The length of Anthropic's contracts is also unique. Typically such contracts last five years or less, while Anthropic's agreement with Amazon extends for twice that amount of time.

"Realistically, anything past three to four years, you're basically throwing darts in the dark," said Corey Quinn, chief cloud economist at spend management software vendor Duckbill. "It is almost a certainty that it will be renegotiated long before the culmination of that contract."

Meanwhile, a data center for Anthropic that starts leasing in 2027 has a 20-year term. For both space and computing, Anthropic is splurging now so it doesn't end up short on capacity long into the future.

"In the past, we have significantly underestimated our compute requirements, which left us capacity constrained and impacted both product development and deployment," Anthropic said in the prospectus.

Anthropic and Amazon didn't provide a comment.

It's a tough time for Anthropic to hit the Nasdaq, with the offering coming as tech investors are desperate for some good news. The Nasdaq is down to start November after dropping 5.3% in October, its worst month since March 2025.

Rising interest rates pushed investors out of debt-heavy neoclouds, which have become a critical piece of the infrastructure buildout. And in the midterm elections earlier this month, Democrats won sweeping victories in both houses of Congress and in key gubernatorial races, while dozens of ballot measures were passed that will potentially limit new data center development across the country.

Then came the Nvidia crisis. Shares of the chipmaker at the center of the AI boom tumbled 13% this week to their lowest since July 2025, after Huang's multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, led to his ouster early Thursday. Huang was replaced on an interim basis with CFO Colette Cress, who faces a major test on Tuesday, two days before the Anthropic debut, as Nvidia is set to report quarterly results.

The ongoing Nvidia fallout and the Anthropic IPO create quite the split-screen moment for tech's heavyweights, which are closely tied to both companies.

Google will be paying particularly close attention.

The company is set to own about 13% of Anthropic after the IPO, and is backstopping the lab's $19 billion in lease commitments to Terawulf, which will supply data center space in Kentucky. If Anthropic goes insolvent, Google will step in and pay the remaining rent or a termination fee, Terawulf said.

"Everybody knows that Anthropic is going to be the future of Google," said Subhadeep Chatterjee, a former Google cloud partnerships leader who now advises companies.

One major impetus for Anthropic to enter costly and long-term guarantees is that OpenAI is being equally aggressive, and capacity is limited, particularly for graphics processing units. Going long can mean locking in rates as the industry confronts questions about whether data center development will slow.

"You will need to be in a rush to get infrastructure, and if you are not going to be there, you will be out of business," said Eli Mansoor, CEO of OskaQ Consulting, which advises companies on cloud negotiations. "It used to be that prices always go down, but this is changing, especially in the GPU world."

Big financial firms are lining up to help Anthropic get what it wants. Bank of America, Blackstone, Citigroup and Morgan Stanley have offered $63 billion to buy racks full of Broadcom AI chips that Anthropic will rent. Broadcom has provided a backstop with a maximum liability of $43 billion.

That's a lot of silicon and a massive bet for a five-year-old company that's never lived through a technology downturn.

"It's not a risk-free prospect, but I would call it smart investing," said David Tollen, founder of Sycamore Legal, where he specializes in IT contracts. "They're investing, in a sense, in themselves. They're putting money into something that if it succeeds, they get a piece of the action."
